## 1.4.257 - 2026-07-28
Two calls from my operator, and one of them was me being wrong. I'd flagged the company's own name
appearing in its own codebase as a sanitization gap — it isn't, and the project had already decided
that months ago in a note I hadn't read carefully enough. Withdrawn. I also confirmed, every way I
know how, that the public repo carries no real credentials.

**Technical:** No code change. Two operator decisions recorded, one self-correction.

**1. FD-05 withdrawn — the `neochrome` finding was re-litigating a settled decision.** I raised it by
reading decision D6 ("full scrub, includes `neochrome` itself") without checking what refined it. The
private denylist carries that rule **deliberately disabled**, reason written inline: *"NeochromeTeam
is the copyright holder's own org and is KEPT (E5)"* and *"Rule retained but disabled so the decision
is visible rather than implicit."* E5 superseded D6 and said so in the one place I would have looked
if I had run the full rule set before reasoning about it.

On the merits it also fails as a security finding:
- `NEOCHROME_TEAM_ID` is a variable **name**; the value is not in the repo. A Slack team ID is not a
  credential — every workspace member sees it and it appears in URLs.
- The tenancy gate is fail-closed; changing the value requires controlling the server environment,
  at which point the gate is moot.
- "The company named Neochrome has a workspace called neochrome" is the first thing anyone would
  guess. Redacting it defends against nothing.

The distinction that does matter, and which I collapsed: redacting `Client A`–`G` protects **third
parties who never consented to being named**. Those 28 rules stay. The vendor's own name is the
vendor's to disclose — and on an AGPL project selling a commercial exception, the vendor is named in
`LICENSE`, `NOTICE` and `LICENSE-COMMERCIAL.md` by design. D6 is now marked superseded in place, with
the original argument kept rather than deleted.

**2. GH-420 closed as an accepted risk** (operator, issue closed manually). `sleuth-app` stays private
permanently and the credential exposure is acceptable for that repo's purposes. Recorded as
**accepted, not rotated** — the keys were not rotated, and the plan says so plainly rather than
implying remediation that did not happen.

**The operator's condition was that the PUBLIC repo must not carry those credentials. Verified three
ways:** the full **60-rule** scan (private client denylist included) over **433 tracked files** →
`✓ CLEAN`; TruffleHog full-tree `verified_secrets: 0`; and every `xoxb-`/`sk-ant-`-shaped string in
the tree identified by hand — two are the scanner's own canary, two are docs quoting it, one is
`sk-ant-your-anthropic-api-key-here` in a config template, one is `sk-ant-test` in a fixture.

`FRONTDOOR.md`: FD-05 → CLOSED (won't fix), **and its check deleted** rather than left to fire
forever — a check that can never go green trains the reader to ignore the block, which is the same
false-precision trap as the pinned count in 1.4.254. Dimension F stays **75**, now with 3 named gaps
instead of 4.

## 1.4.256 - 2026-07-28
I walked my own front door as a total stranger would — cloning the public repo fresh, from scratch,
with no local setup — and found that the very first command in my install instructions was a
placeholder nobody could actually run. Fixed that, fixed the broken "report an issue" links, and left
behind a health board so this gets caught by a command next time instead of by a disappointed newcomer.

**Technical:** Three things, all doc/metadata — no behaviour change.

**1. `/front-door` re-run from a fresh anonymous clone.** Phase 6 of GH-423 called for this and it had
never been done; the plan says it is "the one that counts", because a new repo name at a foreign path
without spaces is the condition an outsider actually hits. Six findings, two fixed immediately:
- **FD-01 FIXED** — `package.json` `repository`/`bugs`/`homepage` all still read `your-org/your-repo`
  on a *published* repo, breaking GitHub's sidebar and "report an issue"
- **FD-02 FIXED** — the first install command was `git clone <your-fork-or-clone-url> sleuth`, which
  fails as written and names the directory `sleuth`. Now the real URL, verified by cloning from it
- **FD-03 OPEN** — `.env.example` documents **1** variable; `src/` reads **31** distinct `process.env`
  keys (11 `SLEUTH_*`). No single place lists what is configurable
- **FD-04 OPEN** — 17 root `*.md` files; 7 internal-process docs the Documentation map never mentions
- **FD-05 OPEN, operator decision** — decision D6 said scrub `neochrome` as a *workspace identifier*
  (D2 deliberately keeps it as the **vendor** in `LICENSE`/`NOTICE`). It survives in **97 files**,
  41 under `PROJECT/`, 8 in `src/` (`NEOCHROME_TEAM_ID`). `ROADMAP.md` names which workspace runs which
  experimental flag — the exact leak D6 was written to prevent. **No credential is exposed**: the
  team-ID value lives outside the repo, and no real Slack team ID appears anywhere in the tree
- **FD-06 OPEN** — no outsider has done a live first run; the fresh-clone test proves it *builds*, which
  is not the same claim

**2. `FRONTDOOR.md`** — the last open Phase 4 item. A board where every status is decided by a command,
invariant **empty output = all green**. Read-only by construction: it never runs the repo's own scripts
or tests, and the expected test count is a hand-updated literal rather than something derived by
executing the suite. **The checks block was executed, not just written** — first extraction produced an
empty file and therefore a silent all-green, which is the same false-green this project has now hit
four times; re-run properly, all six findings fire and every baseline stays silent.

**3. GH-423 plan reconciled with reality.** Phases 0/5/6 still described a world from two days ago —
Phase 5 read "gate not wired", Phase 6 read "name not signed off" and left "flip to public" unticked.
Ticked what shipped, and left **explicitly unticked** the two Phase 6 items that genuinely never ran
(`/front-door` and `/shakedown` re-runs from the fresh clone — the first of which this entry closes).
FD-05 is the kind of thing the checkbox audit exists to surface: a decision recorded as made, only
partially executed.

## 1.4.255 - 2026-07-28
I've updated my dependencies to clear every critical and high-severity security advisory in the code
that actually ships. That included a real one in the email library I use for admin password resets —
a flaw that let a crafted address smuggle extra SMTP commands into a message. Nothing was exploited;
these are fixes for known issues in libraries I depend on, now closed.

**Technical:** Production dependency vulnerabilities **29 -> 12**, with **critical 2 -> 0** and
**high 10 -> 0**. Remaining 12 are all moderate. Two steps:

1. `npm audit fix` (no `--force`) — lockfile-only, `package.json` untouched, 297 insertions /
   542 deletions. Cleared both criticals: `form-data` (CRLF injection via unescaped multipart field
   names; unsafe random boundary selection) and `protobufjs` (arbitrary code execution via code
   injection in generated `toObject` defaults). Also `@grpc/grpc-js`, `fast-uri`, `flatted`,
   `js-yaml`, `jws` (improper HMAC verification), `lodash`, `path-to-regexp`, `tar-fs`, `ws`.
2. `nodemailer` `^8.0.1 -> ^9.0.3`, a deliberate **major** bump — the last remaining production high
   (SMTP command injection via unsanitized `envelope.size`, and CRLF in the transport `name` option).
   Judged low risk on evidence rather than optimism: `src/admin-mailer.js` is the only consumer, and
   it uses the most conservative surface in the library (`createTransport({host, port, secure, auth})`
   + `sendMail({from, to, subject, html})`). v9 declares `engines.node >= 6.0.0`, so it does not
   conflict with our `>= 18.20.4` or CI's pinned 18.20.4.

**Verification, and one gap stated rather than glossed:** jest **1470/1470 across 89 suites**,
`node --test` **30 pass / 0 fail**, `tsc` **50 errors — identical before and after**, measured by
stashing the lockfile and re-running rather than trusting the recorded baseline. But **no test file
covers `admin-mailer`**, so the green suite is *not* evidence for the nodemailer bump. That was
checked separately with a direct smoke test of the exact call shape the code uses.

The "21 high" figure `npm audit` prints without `--omit=dev` is **dev dependencies** (jest and its
tree) and does not ship. Production-only is the number that matters for a published artifact.

## 1.4.254 - 2026-07-28
My secret scanner in CI was only ever checking the files that changed in each commit — not the whole
codebase. On the very first push it checked *nothing at all* and still showed a green tick. I've
fixed that: the full codebase now gets scanned on a schedule and on demand, and I've confirmed it
really does read all of it. Nothing bad was hiding — the full scan came back clean — but a check that
quietly looks at nothing is worse than no check, because it tells you you're safe.

**Technical:** The TruffleHog GitHub Action derives its scan range from the triggering event, so on
`push` and `pull_request` it scans only that event's diff. Measured from the run logs:

| Run | Event | Scanned |
|---|---|---|
| `30318895391` | `push` (initial commit) | **`chunks: 0, bytes: 0`** |
| `30321845101` | `pull_request` | `chunks: 10, bytes: 10436` (4 changed files) |
| `30322218876` | `workflow_dispatch` (after fix) | **`chunks: 751, bytes: 5435279`** |

The zero-byte run is the action's documented behaviour, not a misconfiguration: on a first push
`github.event.before` is the zero SHA, so it sets `BASE=""` but keeps `HEAD=<detached sha>`, and
`--branch <sha>` matches no branch. Result: a gate that passed having examined nothing.

The action already ships a whole-repo path — it resolves `schedule` and `workflow_dispatch` to
`BASE="" HEAD=""` — but neither event was declared, leaving it unreachable. Declared both
(`cron: '0 9 * * 1'` + manual dispatch) and **verified by running it**: 5,435,279 bytes over 751
chunks against 5,430,106 bytes of tracked content, **0 verified secrets**.

Two unverified findings surfaced in the code, both decoys, both deliberately left in place:
- `docs/server-installation-guide.md:288` — `https://your-email:your-token@github.com`, a doc placeholder
- `utils/sanitize-scan.sh:263` — `xoxb-1234567890-9876543210-xxxSECRETxxx`, the scanner's **own canary**,
  the fake token it greps for to prove grep works before it scans anything

Suppressing them was rejected: a scanner configured to ignore its own canary can no longer report
that it is working.

A follow-on effect worth recording, because it invalidated the first fix I wrote: documenting those
two decoys **quoted their literal strings**, so the detectors found the documentation too — the
unverified count went 2 → 8 within one commit. The re-score block originally pinned "expect 2",
which was stale immediately. Replaced with the invariant that actually holds: **the gate is
`verified_secrets: 0`**, and every unverified finding must resolve to a known placeholder in either
documentation or the scanner itself. A pinned count goes stale on any doc edit and trains the
reader to ignore the line.

Also corrected two things that were wrong in the record rather than in the code:
- `GH-423`'s re-score command was `trufflehog filesystem . --results=verified,unknown` while asserting
  "expect 0 unverified" — it filtered `unverified` out of the printout, so the number it told you to
  confirm could not appear. Now `trufflehog git` with `unverified` listed, and the 2 expected decoys
  documented inline. (`filesystem .` also scans `.git/objects`, triple-counting the same strings:
  14.8 MB / 1802 chunks / 7 hits vs 5.4 MB / 751 chunks / 2 hits.)
- The `ci.yml` step comment claimed the action handled "first pushes and empty diffs". That is exactly
  the case where it scanned zero bytes.

Rubric: **G 100 → 75** (named gaps: push/PR still scan diff-only, so whole-tree verified-credential
coverage rides on a cron GitHub disables after 60 days of inactivity; CI still runs 32 of 60 rules).
**I 75 → 100** (repo public, verified anonymously; secret scanning, push protection, Dependabot and
branch protection all enabled). Publication readiness unchanged at 96.5%; **artifact readiness
99% → 96%**. Compensating control for the diff-only gap: `sanitize-scan.sh` reads every tracked file
via `git ls-files -z` on every run.

## 1.4.253 - 2026-07-28
I'm public now — and the README finally explains the thing that would confuse every new reader: the docs say AEGIS but the code, the settings and the install paths all say Sleuth. That's because I was Sleuth internally for three years, and renaming the internals would break every existing install. Now it's written down as a deliberate choice instead of looking like a half-finished rename.

**Technical:** `hiqs-suite/aegis-sleuth-slack-bot` is **public** (verified anonymously — web `200`, `private=false` — not from the authenticated view, which had reported private through an earlier failed attempt).

**New README section: "A note on the name — formerly Sleuth."** A table covering every surface where the old name survives and why each one stayed: the ~34 `SLEUTH_*` environment variables (configuration keys, not branding — renaming breaks every deployed `.env`), code identifiers like `SleuthAuditWriteID` (a purely cosmetic diff that would destroy `git blame` and conflict every open patch), `sleuth-app` paths and systemd unit names (a migration, not a rename), `@Sleuth` in Slack examples (operators name their own app; the code matches mentions of *theirs*), ~888 CHANGELOG entries (rewriting them would make 2023 entries claim a name coined in 2026), and the repo slug carrying both so anyone who knew it as Sleuth can still find it. Summarised as: **AEGIS is the product name, `SLEUTH_`/`sleuth-` is the internal namespace — if a search for AEGIS finds nothing, search for Sleuth.** Signposted three ways: a heads-up in the intro, a TOC entry, and a callout at the top of Configuration reference, where a reader first meets `SLEUTH_`.

**Fixed a defect I introduced:** the README had **two `## Security` sections**. The second was added alongside `SECURITY.md` and duplicated a richer existing one — and because both rendered as `#security`, the table-of-contents anchor silently resolved to the wrong one. Removed the duplicate and folded the `SECURITY.md` pointer into the surviving section.

**Protections enabled now that they are possible** — on a free org these are unavailable while a repo is private, which is why the plan's "protect then flip" order had to invert: secret scanning **enabled**, push protection **enabled**, Dependabot security updates **enabled**, and branch protection on `main` with required check `test`, `enforce_admins`, no force-pushes, no deletions. That last one also means the repo is no longer amend-and-force-push territory; changes now go through pull requests. [README.md](README.md).

## 1.4.252 - 2026-07-27
Both of my safety checks now actually run, on a real machine, and both pass. That sounds small, but running them for the first time is what found the two bugs in the previous two entries — neither of which any amount of reading the code had caught. I'm sitting in the new repository, private, waiting on one human decision to become public.

**Technical:** GH-423 Phase 6 executed up to (and deliberately not through) the public flip.

**What landed.** `hiqs-suite/aegis-sleuth-slack-bot`: **private**, **1 commit**, 432 files, author *and* committer `Neochrome <devops@neochro.me>` — both set, because `--author` alone does not change the committer and in a zero-history repo commit #1's identity is permanent. Verified from a **real fresh clone** at a foreign path: `npm ci` exit 0, jest **1470/1470**, `npm run hooks:install` wired `core.hooksPath`, no local-path leaks. The pre-commit hook was then given a real staged Slack-shaped token and **refused the commit**.

**CI run `30318895391`: all 7 steps green** — jest, `secret + PII scan`, TruffleHog verified, TruffleHog unverified. That is the first fully-passing execution of gates that had been "built and reviewed" since earlier today.

**Two deviations from the plan worth recording.** (1) GH-423 Phase 6 assumed protections go on *before* the flip. On a free org they cannot: secret scanning returns `422 not available` and branch protection `403 Upgrade to GitHub Pro or make this repository public`. Both are free **for public repos**, so the order must invert to flip-then-protect, leaving a brief unprotected window. (2) The public tree carries **431→432 files, not 433** — the two relay threads from the swarm test are excluded because `relay-system/` is gitignored under decision E8, which is correct.

**Rubric.** **C 75 → 100** (fresh-clone proof), **G 75 → 100** (both gates executed and passed), **I 0 → 75** (created, pushed, verified; not public). Publication **84% → 96.5%**, artifact **93% → 99%**. The remaining 3.5% is one irreversible action.

**Still open for the operator, and worth deciding before the flip:** `npm audit` reports **29 production vulnerabilities (2 critical, 10 high)** including a direct dependency, and GitHub publishes Dependabot alerts on public repos. Not a leak, not a blocker — but cheap to address now and visible later. [PROJECT/1-INBOX/GH-423-PUBLIC-REPO-CUTOVER.md](PROJECT/1-INBOX/GH-423-PUBLIC-REPO-CUTOVER.md).

## 1.4.251 - 2026-07-27
My second secret scanner was pinned to a version of itself that doesn't exist, so it crashed instead of scanning. Caught by actually running it. Fixed.

**Technical:** `ci.yml` pinned `version: v3.96.0` for the TruffleHog step. That input is the **Docker image tag on ghcr.io**, which carries **no `v` prefix** — the job died with `manifest unknown`, exit 125, before scanning anything. Verified against the registry rather than assumed: `ghcr.io/trufflesecurity/trufflehog:v3.96.0` → **404**, `:3.96.0` → **200**. Now `version: "3.96.0"`, with a comment recording the distinction.

**This was a reviewer miss, and a specific kind.** During the swarm review I *raised* the version pin as finding S2 and had it changed from 3.95.2 to 3.96.0 — then verified `v3.96.0` through the GitHub **releases** API, where it genuinely exists. But `uses:` and `version:` resolve against two different namespaces that look identical: the action ref is a git tag (v-prefixed, pinned here to SHA `6f3c981e`, still correct), while `version:` is a container tag (not v-prefixed). Verifying the right-looking string in the wrong registry is indistinguishable from verifying nothing, and no amount of re-reading the YAML would have caught it — only executing it did.

**Both CI failures so far were found by running the gate, not reviewing it**, on a workflow that had passed a two-round adversarial review. The first was the scanner's non-portable binary detection; this is the second. Gates: YAML re-parsed (7 steps, both TruffleHog steps `version=3.96.0`), 60-rule scan **CLEAN**, jest **1470/1470**. [.github/workflows/ci.yml](.github/workflows/ci.yml).

## 1.4.250 - 2026-07-27
My own secret scanner had a blind spot, and it took running it on a real Linux CI machine for the first time to find it. On a Mac it was quietly skipping a source file and still telling me everything was clean. Nothing was actually leaked — I checked that file by hand — but a check that reports "all clear" when it hasn't looked is worse than no check at all, so that's fixed and it now behaves the same on every machine.

**Technical:** Found by the **first real CI run** of the gate — the exact scenario the readiness rubric refused to score as verified until it had executed in anger. CI failed on `secret + PII scan` while the identical command exited `0` locally on the identical tree.

**Root cause: `grep -IL ''` is not portable.** GNU grep lists a NUL-containing file as non-matching so `-L` reports it; BSD grep on macOS does not. Same tree, same command: macOS printed `binary/unreadable files needing manual review: 0` and exited `0`; Linux found `1` and exited `1`. The scanner's own comment says *"no findings on a binary means 'not examined', never 'clean'"* — and on macOS that safety net had been silently inoperative. **A false green inside the mechanism built to prevent false greens.** Detection now tests for NUL directly with `perl -0777 -ne 'exit(/\x00/ ? 0 : 1)'`: identical on every platform, and it is the actual definition `grep -I` was approximating.

**The file it was hiding was not corrupt.** `src/channel-membership-cache.js` contains exactly 2 NUL bytes, both deliberate — a composite cache key uses NUL as the separator between channel and user id because it cannot occur in either component (`` const Key = `${ArgChannelId}\0${ArgUserId}` ``). It parses, it is tested, and it is legitimate. But it had **never been scanned**, so it was checked by hand against every credential shape the scanner looks for — Slack `xoxb`/`xoxp`/`xapp`, OpenAI `sk-`, GitHub `ghp_`, AWS `AKIA`, PEM blocks, bearer tokens, IPv4 literals. **No matches.**

**New:** `utils/sanitize-reviewed-binaries.txt` — a reviewed-binaries record, so a legitimately NUL-containing *source* file can be accepted without disabling the audit. Entries require a reviewer, a date, and what was checked; an entry without evidence is a suppressed alarm, not a review. A missing or empty file means "nothing reviewed", which correctly keeps the gate **closed**. Verified with a negative test: an unreviewed NUL-containing file still fails the gate (`exit 1`) while the reviewed one passes.

**Why this matters more than the bug.** The gate that was red is the one guarding a public release, and the failure mode was the one that gets gates deleted: red on a demonstrably clean tree. Had CI not run before the repo went public, every macOS "CLEAN" would have kept carrying an unexamined file. Gates: full 60-rule scan **CLEAN**, jest **1470/1470 across 89 suites**, `bash -n` clean. [utils/sanitize-scan.sh](utils/sanitize-scan.sh), [utils/sanitize-reviewed-binaries.txt](utils/sanitize-reviewed-binaries.txt).

## 1.4.249 - 2026-07-27
I have a new name: **AEGIS**. Everything an outside reader sees now says AEGIS, but my internals deliberately still say Sleuth — the settings you've configured, the paths on your server, and the way you @-mention me in Slack are all unchanged. Renaming those would have broken every running install for no benefit, so I left them alone and said so out loud rather than quietly half-renaming myself.

**Technical:** Product renamed **Sleuth → AEGIS** ahead of publication to `hiqs-suite/aegis-sleuth-slack-bot`. Scoped to the **product name only** by explicit decision: **149 replacements across 25 product-facing docs** (README, NOTICE, LICENSE-COMMERCIAL, THIRD-PARTY, CONTRIBUTING, SECURITY, HONEST, AGENTS, ARCHITECTURE, macos-install-guide, `docs/*`), plus `package.json` `name` → `aegis`.

**What was deliberately NOT renamed, and why.** The 34 `SLEUTH_*` environment variables, all `sleuth-app` paths and service names, code identifiers such as `SleuthAuditWriteID`, and `@Sleuth` — the last of which is **functional in 18 source files and 4 tests**, so renaming it in docs alone would have made the docs lie about the code. Renaming env vars would additionally be a breaking change against a hand-managed `.env.runtime`, and would fork this tree permanently from the private repo that E11 keeps active. The repo slug itself keeps `sleuth`, so this is a transitional identity rather than an erasure — the docs are consistent with that.

**The replacement rule was `(?<!@)\bSleuth\b`, and the word boundary is load-bearing.** It leaves `SleuthAuditWriteID` untouched (no boundary before a capital) and every lowercase `sleuth-app` path untouched (case-sensitive), while still catching prose compounds like `Sleuth-owned`. Verified by assertion, not by eye: 149 before → **0 after**, 25 files changed, with an automatic revert wired in if any of those three failed. `LICENSE` was excluded outright and re-confirmed at 0 mentions — the AGPL text stays verbatim.

**A silent-zero bug nearly hid the whole thing.** The first dry run reported **0 replacements** across all 32 files while README alone demonstrably had 20. Root cause: this shell has a customised `IFS` that does not split on spaces, so `for f in $FILES` iterated **once** with the entire list as a single path; `[ -f "$f" ]` then failed and `continue` fired — no error, just a zero. Same family as the `git grep` silent-zero recorded earlier in this project. Fixed with an explicit bash array. Had the zero been trusted, the release would have shipped a completely un-renamed tree while reporting success.

**CHANGELOG history left as Sleuth by decision** — entries predating July 2026 use the name the product actually had; rewriting 888 entries would make 2023 entries claim a name coined in 2026. The framing note now states the rename and explains why the `SLEUTH_` prefix survives.

**Rubric.** Dimension **H (identity) 0 → 100**: org `hiqs-suite` (operator is admin, already OAuth-authorized), repo created **private and empty**. Publication **76.5% → 81.5%**; artifact unchanged at **90%**. Gates: sanitize-scan **CLEAN** (60 rules), jest **1470/1470 across 89 suites**. **Nothing has been pushed to the new repo.** [README.md](README.md), [NOTICE](NOTICE).

## 1.4.248 - 2026-07-27
I now check myself for leaked secrets in two more places: automatically on every pull request, and locally *before* a commit is even created. That second one matters most — once a credential lands in a commit, deleting it doesn't unexpose it, so catching it a second earlier is worth more than catching it a minute later. Both were built by two AI agents working as builders while a third reviewed their work, and the review caught six real defects the builders' own reports had described as working.

**Technical:** GH-423 Phase 5. Two lanes driven through the XYZ relay harness — **codex** and **agy** as headless builders, Claude as orchestrator/reviewer, each lane scoped to an explicit write-set.

**Lane 1 — TruffleHog in CI (`ci.yml`), approved in 2 rounds.** Two passes, both pinned to the commit SHA `6f3c981e7b77f235fd2702dd74af25fc4b72bf11` — **verified against the GitHub API** as `v3.96.0` rather than trusting the comment, and deliberately the same version that cleared the RC (`moved-files.md:105-107`), since detector sets change between releases and CI scanning with an older set than the one that certified the artifact inverts the point. Verified findings fail the build; unverified are surfaced via `continue-on-error` so a noisy gate doesn't get disabled. **The blocker:** codex passed explicit `base:`/`head:` inputs, which bypasses the action's own event handling — including its zero-SHA special case. `github.event.before` is all zeros on a branch's first push, and `git rev-parse` *succeeds* on a well-formed-but-nonexistent SHA (verified: exit 0, while `cat-file` fails), so the action's own guard can't catch it. Fix was subtractive: delete the inputs and the guards, let the action resolve the event. Note the reviewer's own worked example was wrong — `ci.yml` only triggers on push to main/development, so the real scenario is the **first push to main in the new repo**, i.e. Phase 6's very first CI run.

**Lane 2 — pre-commit hook, approved in 3 rounds + 1 aborted.** `.githooks/pre-commit`, `utils/install-git-hooks.sh`, `npm run hooks:install`; wired via `core.hooksPath`, never by copying into `.git/hooks`. Four defects: the hook omitted `--allowlist` and so produced **44 findings on a demonstrably clean tree** (the builder reported this as "the blocked case working"); two of three write-set files were described as written but did not exist; `core.hooksPath` was set by hand instead of by the missing installer, discovered when it blocked the reviewer's own commit; and every non-zero scanner exit was labelled "Secrets were found… you may bypass" — including exit **2**, which means *the scanner could not verify anything*. That last one invited a bypass at the exact moment nothing had been scanned. All three exit paths now executed and verified: clean `0`, cannot-vouch `2` with an explicit do-NOT-bypass, missing-scanner `2`.

**One defect was the orchestrator's.** `ALLOW_PATHS` is comma-separated (`agy-turn.sh:37`); it was passed space-separated, so it parsed as a single nonexistent path and the harness correctly reverted a perfectly good builder turn. Lane 1 survived only because it had one path and never exercised the separator. Work was recovered from `.tick/orphan-backups/`. Containment behaved exactly as designed — reverted, backed up, failed loudly rather than committing a turn it couldn't vouch for.

**Rubric.** Dimension **G 40 → 75**; publication **73% → 76.5%**, artifact **86% → 90%**. Deliberately **not 100**: neither gate has executed in anger — no GitHub Actions run has happened, `core.hooksPath` is currently unset here, and CI still runs shape rules only (32 of 60) because the literal denylist is itself the de-anonymization key and cannot ship. Three of the six defects were found by *running* the artifact rather than reading its description, which is the whole case for the reviewer split. [.github/workflows/ci.yml](.github/workflows/ci.yml), [.githooks/pre-commit](.githooks/pre-commit), [utils/install-git-hooks.sh](utils/install-git-hooks.sh).

## 1.4.247 - 2026-07-27
I now have the two documents every open-source project needs and most write badly: how to contribute, and how to tell us about a security hole. The security one names the single most dangerous thing about running me — if you don't set an API token, I fall back to the literal password `test` on an interface that creates workspaces and accepts credentials. That's in the install instructions now, not buried in a hardening guide.

**Technical:** GH-423 Phase 3 **complete (7/7)**. `CONTRIBUTING.md` and `SECURITY.md` added; `LICENSE-COMMERCIAL.md` and README updated to reference them.

**The CLA question is answered, and it was the one-way door.** Resolved as a **lightweight inbound licence grant, not a signed CLA**: contributors keep their copyright and license under the AGPL like everyone else, *and* additionally grant Neochrome the right to include the work under other terms. Agreement is recorded by the act of opening a PR — no signing bot, which would be disproportionate at this size. **A DCO was considered and rejected with the reason written down:** a DCO certifies provenance but grants only the project's outbound licence (AGPL), which is precisely what would make a contribution unusable in a commercially licensed build. The extra grant is the smallest addition that keeps dual-licensing viable. Upgrade path noted: a signature-tracked CLA can replace it later, applying to future contributions only. This mattered to settle *before* publication — a PR merged under the wrong inbound terms cannot be un-merged out of a commercial build.

**`SECURITY.md` states the real posture rather than an aspirational one.** Private reporting to `security@neochro.me` (already in the tree, so it demonstrably routes); response windows given as 5/10 business days rather than a 24-hour SLA a small team cannot honour; latest-version-only support, since Sleuth is source-distributed and each operator runs their own deployment; explicit in/out scope. The **operator responsibilities** section names the `WEB_API_BEARER_TOKEN` → `test` fallback outright rather than describing it as "a well-known default" — verified verbatim against `src/app.js#GetWebApiBearerToken` (lines 90-107), whose own comment already calls it "a full credential-injection path". Framed as part of installation, not hardening, and surfaced in the README's new Security section for the same reason. `.env` gitignored / `.env.example` placeholder-only re-confirmed; rotate-then-purge stated as the remedy for any committed credential, because deleting it from the repo does not unexpose it.

**Rubric.** Dimension **E (legal & licensing) 70 → 100**; publication readiness **69% → 73%**, artifact **81% → 86%**. Full trajectory `71/84 → 69/81 → 73/86` — the middle step is the checkbox audit moving the number *down*, and dimension **B** still carries that correction. Gates: sanitize-scan **CLEAN** (60 rules), jest **1470/1470 across 89 suites**. [CONTRIBUTING.md](CONTRIBUTING.md), [SECURITY.md](SECURITY.md).

## 1.4.246 - 2026-07-26
I finally have a licence. I'm open source under the AGPL — you can run me, change me, and self-host me for free, forever. The one string attached: if you modify me and then let *other* people use your modified version over a network, you have to share your changes with them. If that doesn't work for your business, there's a commercial licence you can buy instead. I also checked all 616 of the libraries I'm built from to make sure none of them conflict with that choice. None do.

**Technical:** GH-423 Phase 3 (licence and legal files). `LICENSE` is the verbatim AGPL-3.0 text — **not** typed from memory: fetched from two independent upstream projects (Mastodon, Nextcloud) and confirmed byte-identical, sha1 `78e50e18`, 661 lines, §13 present at line 540. `package.json` `license` → `AGPL-3.0-only`. `NOTICE` carries the copyright, the SPDX identifier, the dual-licence statement, and an explicit trademark exclusion. `LICENSE-COMMERCIAL.md` states what the commercial option grants (relief from the §13 network-source obligation and from AGPL-licensing derivative works), what it does *not* grant (trademarks, any change to dependency licensing), and that terms are negotiated rather than click-through. README licence section rewritten around the actual decision point most readers face: unmodified use triggers nothing extra, §13 is what to know about, commercial exists if §13 doesn't work for you.

**Dependency audit — this was a gate, not a footnote.** AGPL-3.0 can be blocked outright by a single GPL-incompatible dependency, so `THIRD-PARTY.md` records the audit before publication rather than after. **616 packages total (352 distributed), zero copyleft or source-available licences** — no GPL, LGPL, AGPL, MPL, EPL, CDDL, SSPL, BUSL, or Commons Clause anywhere. All 12 direct production dependencies are MIT / MIT-0 / Apache-2.0. Four cases were checked individually rather than waved through: Apache-2.0 (27 packages incl. `newrelic` and `openai`) is one-way compatible **into** the GPLv3 family, which is the direction that matters since Sleuth is AGPL-3.0-only; `argparse@2.0.1` declares Python-2.0, which the FSF classifies GPL-compatible (only the historical 1.6b1 licence was not); `caniuse-lite` is CC-BY-4.0 *data* in the dev toolchain, not distributed code; and the `MIT*`/`Apache*` entries are `license-checker` flagging a licence read from a `LICENSE` file rather than declared in `package.json`, not an unknown licence. Regeneration commands are in the doc.

**Superseded.** An earlier commit on this branch (`2f00ae6`) licensed the repo Apache-2.0; this reverses it back to GH-423 decision D1 before anything was published. **Still open and now load-bearing:** inbound contributions need a CLA — selling a commercial exception requires holding rights to all the code, and a pull request accepted under the AGPL alone cannot go into a commercially licensed build. `LICENSE-COMMERCIAL.md` states plainly that external PRs are not being merged until `CONTRIBUTING.md` settles this. Per-file `SPDX-License-Identifier` headers were **not** added: no source file in this repo carries one today, so the project convention doesn't call for them; the identifier lives in `NOTICE` and `package.json`. Gates re-run green: sanitize-scan **CLEAN** (60 rules, 425 files), jest **1470/1470 across 89 suites**. [LICENSE](LICENSE), [NOTICE](NOTICE), [LICENSE-COMMERCIAL.md](LICENSE-COMMERCIAL.md), [THIRD-PARTY.md](THIRD-PARTY.md).

## 1.4.245 - 2026-07-26
I've been scrubbed clean so I can be shared publicly. Every client name, production server address, Slack ID, teammate's name and local file path in my code and docs has been swapped for a generic stand-in, and the private working files have been moved somewhere they can't be published by accident. My README is now written for someone who has never met me, rather than for the people who built me. Two independent secret scanners now agree I'm clean — and one of them turned up something important: an API key that was fixed in my README months ago was still sitting *inside* my search index, and it's still live. That one needs rotating today; it doesn't affect the public copy, but it's real.

**Technical:** GH-423 Phases 1–2 executed on `public/release-candidate-01`. Approach diverges from GH-423's allowlist-copy plan: sanitized **in place** so every redaction is a reviewable diff, with a deterministic scanner as the compensating gate for the denylist risk. Tracked files **551 → 441**.

**Tooling (both ship).** `utils/sanitize-scan.sh` — 59 rules, hardcoded `/usr/bin/grep`, canary self-test, exits `2` (never `0`) if it cannot verify its own tooling. Motivating defect: `git grep` is intercepted on this machine and **silently returns zero results**, so the first sweep reported no IP addresses while three live production IPs sat in nine files. `utils/sanitize-apply.sh` — ordered replacement engine; order is load-bearing because these identifiers nest. Rules split by sensitivity: generic shape rules ship, the literal denylist (real client names, prod IPs, Slack IDs) is itself the de-anonymization key and stays private. `utils/sanitize-allowlist.txt` records every match accepted by decision, with its reason.

**Quarantined, not deleted** (all moved to a gitignored folder, logged with reversal instructions): `relay-system/` (47 files of raw AI reviewer transcripts), `phases/` (50), `data/rag/sleuth-rag.sqlite` (5.3 MB — a searchable index of the entire codebase; **it was tracked**), four production reminder dumps, and 11 internal-only root docs. Note `temp/` was gitignored but **gitignore does not apply to already-tracked files**, so those dumps were shipping regardless.

**Redacted in place** (~1,000 replacements): 7 client names → `Client A`–`G` with a two-column prose/identifier convention; 3 production IPs → RFC 5737 TEST-NET-3; a real SSH public key; 19 Slack channel/user/list IDs; absolute local paths → `$REPO_ROOT`/`$HOME` (which leaked the operator's username *and* broke for outside users); and real teammates' names, found by reading docs rather than by any scanner — there is no regex for "is this a person". `config/servers.json`, `client-channel-mapping.json` and `remote-rag-projects.json` were sanitized in place rather than removed, since the app reads them.

**Two false greens caught, both worth knowing.** (1) The applier reported "945 replacements" and wrote **nothing**: its `EXIT` trap fired when a command-substitution *subshell* exited, deleting the scratch dir mid-run. It now asserts its inputs before writing and hard-fails when a non-zero count yields zero changed files. (2) A checkpoint reported "1449 pass / 0 fail" while **two suites were failing to parse** — a suite that fails to run contributes zero passes *and* zero fails, so the summary looked perfect. Root cause was the predicted identifier-shape hazard landing in code: the prose rule produced `const Client AClient = {}`, a space inside a JS identifier. `node --check` over every tracked `.js`/`.mjs` is now a standing gate. Verified against a `git archive` of `development`: baseline is **1551 tests / 93 suites**, and this branch matches exactly.

**Security.** Removed a hardcoded **New Relic license key** plus a `NRAK-` user key from `newrelic.js` (monitoring is now opt-in, no fallback — a committed default reports your telemetry into someone else's account); the scanner had no rule for those shapes, so 10 more provider formats were backfilled. `README.md` credential examples are placeholders only — that file leaked four live keys. `WEB_API_BEARER_TOKEN` unset still falls back to the literal token `test` on an API that creates workspaces and accepts credentials; **behaviour deliberately unchanged** (breaking existing deployments is out of scope) but the startup warning is now impossible to miss and the README leads with it. Tracked by GH-420.

**Reviews.** An `agy` `/relay-xyz` round returned **FAIL** with 2 blockers, both real and both fixed: the allowlist matched *substrings*, so `xxx+` silently swallowed a live-shaped token containing "xxx" (reproduced before fixing, now anchored); and lockfiles were skipped despite being a private-registry leak vector. It also caught that the squashed commit carries the committer's identity — Phase 6 now sets `--author` *and* `GIT_COMMITTER_*`. `/front-door` found a second competing onboarding door carrying a named Product Owner and internal KPIs, plus `package.json` `main` pointing at a file that does not exist. `/shakedown` found both installers hardcoding a repo URL in the old org — a guaranteed 404 on step one for every outside user; now driven by `SLEUTH_REPO`/`SLEUTH_REPO_URL`.

**Final gates.** `sanitize-scan.sh`: **CLEAN**. **TruffleHog 3.96.0** over the publishable artifact and over a simulated zero-history squash: **0 verified, 0 unverified** on both. App boots from placeholder config; `tsc` at parity with baseline (50, no net-new). **TruffleHog over the existing private history found 14 VERIFIED secrets** — it authenticated the GH-420 OpenAI key against OpenAI's API, so that key is **live right now**. 14 of 17 hits are inside the quarantined RAG SQLite index, which had ingested the README while it still held the key: `grep -I` treats binaries as non-matching, so our own scanner could never have seen it. That is why the binary audit now fails the gate rather than staying silent, and why two independently-built scanners are required. The public RC is unaffected (zero-history); **rotation is the only remedy** and is owned by GH-420. [package.json](package.json).

Codex returned "Changes requested" with 3 `[Should]` findings, all fixed here: **(1)** `thread-memory.js#CaptureThreadAsync` resolved the same mentioned user once per message instead of once per thread capture, despite already maintaining a `DisplayNameCache` for sender names right next to the new call — `ResolveMentionsForExternalDisplayAsync` (`src/slack-message-pipeline.js`) now takes an optional caller-owned `Map` cache (defaults to a fresh one), and `CaptureThreadAsync` passes its existing `DisplayNameCache` straight through since both are the same "user id → display name, fallback to id" lookup. **(2)** the mention-token regex (`<@ID>` / `<@ID|label>`) existed as three independent copies — `slack-message-pipeline.js`'s own `.replace()`, `SlackFormatUtils.ExtractUserMentions`'s internal pattern, and a third inline `matchAll` variant in `web-api.js#BuildRebalanceReminderExportAsync`. Consolidated: `SlackFormatUtils` now owns one private `#MENTION_TOKEN_PATTERN`, used by both `ExtractUserMentions` (existing) and a new `ReplaceUserMentions(text, resolveFn)` (each call wraps it in `new RegExp(...)` to avoid `g`-flag `lastIndex` state leaking across calls); `slack-message-pipeline.js` now calls `ReplaceUserMentions` instead of its own literal, and `web-api.js` now calls `ExtractUserMentions` instead of its bespoke regex. Per Codex's explicit recommendation, did *not* merge network-backed resolution into the pure `SlackFormatUtils`, and did *not* depend on `web-api.js`'s private `#ResolveUserDisplayNameAsync` (not shareable) — only its caller-owned-cache idiom was reused. **(3)** the "aspirational unified pipeline" module comment oversold what's actually built (one function, two call sites) — added an explicit "Current status" note to `slack-message-pipeline.js`'s JSDoc scoping the claim (operator call: keep the aspirational name, just be honest in the comment about current scope rather than renaming). 11 new regression tests (`ReplaceUserMentions` + a `lastIndex`-leak regression in `tests/slack-format-utils.test.js`; shared-cache dedup cases in `tests/slack-message-pipeline.test.js`; a 3-mentions-1-lookup case in `tests/thread-memory.test.js`, spy-verified via `jest.spyOn`). Full suite green (1550/1550). [package.json](package.json).

## 1.4.242 - 2026-07-23
GH-428 was a bug report I filed on myself: the message I quoted in it still showed raw Slack IDs like `@U08BHQEAX` instead of names, and the "View Slack message" link went to a blank page. Both are fixed — the report now shows real names, and I've made the link more reliable too.

**Technical:** GH-428 follow-through, two independent fixes plus a new shared building block. **(1) Unresolved mentions (GH-429):** `chat-module.js#HandleBugReportReactionAsync` and `thread-memory.js#CaptureThreadAsync` passed raw Slack message text (with `<@U...>` mention tokens) straight into a filed GitHub issue's title/body/transcript. Slack's own client resolves those tokens to display names automatically; GitHub's markdown does not. Added `src/slack-message-pipeline.js` (`ResolveMentionsForExternalDisplayAsync`) as the seed of an aspirational unified "make Slack text safe to read outside Slack" pipeline — resolves each distinct mentioned user id once via the existing `GetUserDisplayNameAsync`, falling back to the raw id on lookup failure so nothing silently disappears. Wired into both call sites; `thread-memory.js#CaptureThreadAsync` now resolves inline mentions *within* each captured message body, not just the sender's own display-name line prefix. Other Slack-text-leaves-Slack call sites (`web-api.js`'s rebalance export, `github-comment-relay.js`) were identified but deliberately left for a future pass — see GH-429 for the tracked list. **(2) Blank "View Slack message" link (GH-430):** traced to `GetPermaLinkAsync` (`src/slack-app.js`) passing through `chat.getPermalink`'s response verbatim; that response can come back wrapped in a `/?redir=%2Farchives%2F...` auth-bounce form that renders blank for some browser/session combinations (not signed into that workspace in that tab, third-party cookies blocked, etc.) — confirmed via full-repo search that no `redir=` construction exists in this codebase, so this is Slack's own web client, not a bug in our URL building. Hardened rather than root-caused: `GetPermaLinkAsync` still calls `chat.getPermalink` to gate access/existence, but now builds its own canonical `https://<workspace>.slack.com/archives/<channel>/p<ts>[?thread_ts=...&cid=...]` URL from data already available locally, sidestepping the redir wrapper regardless of cause; `chat-module.js`'s `:bug:`-reaction flow now passes the thread root ts through for correct thread deep-linking. 16 new regression tests: `tests/slack-message-pipeline.test.js` (9, resolver unit coverage), `tests/slack-app-permalink.test.js` (5, reproduces the exact redir-wrapped response + access-gate cases), plus 1 new case each in `tests/chat-module-bug-reaction.test.js` and `tests/thread-memory.test.js` reproducing GH-428's exact message shape. Full suite green (1539/1539; the 19 `EPERM: operation not permitted` failures seen under the sandboxed dev harness are a pre-existing local port-binding restriction, confirmed passing unsandboxed). [package.json](package.json).

## 1.4.241 - 2026-07-21
Had Codex review the v1.4.240 work headlessly (no live Slack, no human relay) and it earned its keep: it caught a real blocker I'd missed — the new test harness I just shipped would have crashed the instant anyone actually ran it the documented way — plus a couple of legitimate hygiene gaps. All fixed, and I corrected a test-count I'd gotten wrong in the previous entry too.

**Technical:** Drove a headless Codex review (`relay-xyz` skill, round-cap 1) of the 1.4.240 diff. Findings, all confirmed by direct reproduction before fixing: **[Blocker]** `scripts/reminder-thread-battery.js`'s only documented invocation is plain `node`, but `tests/mocks/mock-slack-app.js`'s constructor calls `jest.fn()` unconditionally — under real `node` (no `global.jest`) this threw `jest is not defined` immediately, confirmed via a fresh `node -e` repro before the fix and after. Fixed by installing the same minimal jest shim `scripts/first-time-user-battery.js` already uses, guarded so it never overwrites a real Jest environment. **[Should]** a turn whose `SimulateMessageAsync` call threw was caught, logged, and then swallowed — `MainAsync` still called `process.exit(0)`, so a broken pipeline read as green to any calling script/CI. Fixed: turn errors now accumulate and `RunScenarioAsync` throws after cleanup if any occurred, so `MainAsync`'s existing catch-and-`exit(1)` path fires for real. **[Should]** the throwaway `<workspace>-test-harness` name was fixed (collision risk across concurrent runs) and cleanup missed a fifth persistence file `RemindersModule`'s `EventStore` also writes — `data/runtime/events/<workspace>_events.jsonl`. Fixed: workspace name is now pid-suffixed, cleanup runs before *and* after each invocation, and the event ledger is included. **[Should]** the 1.4.240 entry above claimed 22 new regression tests; actual count is 21 (6 handler + 15 harness, after this pass added 2 more: a real child-process repro of the jest-shim blocker, and a spied-failure test for the new error-propagation path) — corrected in place since that entry hadn't shipped anywhere yet. Full suite green. Process note: the relay itself hit its own infrastructure snag — the vendored `.xyz` harness's driven-turn `tick release` resolved a different `TICK_REPO_ROOT` than the one tracking the real token, so two consecutive drive attempts produced full, real review content in the transcript that never actually landed as a commit; recovered by reading the raw transcript log directly rather than re-running a third time. Filed as a harness bug via `/file-xyz-bug` rather than reverse-engineering the vendored scripts further.

## 1.4.240 - 2026-07-21
The narrower fix from v1.4.239 turned out to be a whack-a-mole — "please see above" still didn't work, since that's a different verb than "follow on above". So instead of chasing every new verb one at a time, I now treat the standalone word "above" itself as the trigger: any thread reply containing "above" plus a real time (any phrasing — "see above," "check above," "per above," anything) now pulls in the earlier messages before I write the reminder. I also got a new internal tool that replays a scripted Slack conversation against my real logic (no live Slack needed) so this kind of thing can be checked turn-by-turn before it ships.

**Technical:** GH-424 continued. `scripts/slack-harness-post.js` cannot be used to test the message-event reminder pipeline at all — it posts via the live bot token, and Slack Bolt's default `ignoreSelf: true` middleware silently drops any event authored by the bot's own user ID before any handler (including `RemindersModule#OnMessageAsync`) ever sees it. Added `scripts/reminder-thread-battery.js` (+ `tests/reminder-thread-battery.test.js`), modeled on the existing `scripts/first-time-user-battery.js` pattern: drives the real `RemindersModule`/`WorkspaceAI` through `MockSlackApp.SimulateMessageAsync` (no Slack transport, no `ignoreSelf` issue), replaying a scripted multi-turn thread and printing Sleuth's actual reply + any scheduled reminder after each turn. Runs against a throwaway `<workspace>-test-harness` workspace name so its disk-backed reminder persistence never touches the real workspace's runtime files. Using this harness immediately surfaced that "please see above and meet at the park at 2 PM tomorrow" still failed post-1.4.239, confirming `TASK_ABOVE_SHORTHAND_PATTERN`'s enumerated verb list (`do|handle|complete|...|follow (up) on|knock out`) is a structurally losing approach — each new phrasing needs its own regex entry. Added `ABOVE_REFERENCE_PATTERN` (`/\babove\b/i`) as a third trigger alongside the existing pronoun patterns in `TryEnrichVagueCompletionFromAboveAsync` (which already prepends the last 3 preceding human thread messages for "it"/"this"/"that" references) — deliberately verb-agnostic, since "above" is far less ambiguous than those pronouns and needs no preposition/verb guard. `TASK_ABOVE_SHORTHAND_PATTERN` (still verb-enumerated) keeps first crack at matching for its explicit-mention-targeting + no-time-default behavior; the new pattern is the fallback for everything else. 21 new regression tests (6 in `tests/reminders-app-mention-handler.test.js` across both new phrasings and edge cases — no-schedule-trigger, no-preceding-message — plus 15 in the new harness's own test file). Also fixed real test-infra flakiness discovered while building the harness: `RemindersModule.StartAsync` unconditionally schedules a daily-digest + weekly-report `setTimeout` (the weekly one isn't cleared by `StopAsync` at all — a latent pre-existing gap), and their wall-clock-computed delay can floor to ~1s, occasionally firing during/after a test and racing unrelated test files sharing the same jest worker (manifested as intermittent unrelated failures in `web-api-reminders.test.js`/`chat-module.integration.test.js` — reproduced and bisected to this harness specifically, confirmed absent on a 6-run clean baseline). Fixed by wrapping the harness's `RunScenarioAsync` describe block in `jest.useFakeTimers()`/`useRealTimers()` rather than touching the production scheduler. Separately observed (not a regression): `tests/web-api-workspace.test.js` can intermittently fail with `fetch failed` under rapid repeated full-suite runs in this sandboxed dev environment — matches a previously-documented sandbox port-binding gotcha, unrelated to this change. Full suite green (1521/1521), confirmed clean across 20+ repeated runs.

## 1.4.239 - 2026-07-21
I fixed a bug where saying "please help follow on above" in a thread reply wouldn't pull in the earlier messages — the reminder I created literally said "above" instead of the actual task, because I didn't recognize that phrasing as a request to look upward in the thread.

**Technical:** GH-424. `TASK_ABOVE_SHORTHAND_PATTERN` in `src/reminders-app-mention-handler.js` only matched the verb phrase `follow up on` before `above`, not the shorter `follow on` variant reported in the bug (`@Alex Rivera please help follow on above with WP Engine support chat by 11 AM PT`). When the shorthand regex failed to match, `RemindersModule#OnMessageAsync` fell through past `TryHandleTaskAboveShorthandAsync` and `TryEnrichVagueCompletionFromAboveAsync` (neither pattern applies to the literal word "above") straight to the plain message-event auto-schedule path, which schedules from the raw message text alone with no preceding thread content — so the AI had nothing to resolve "above" against and echoed it verbatim into the reminder title. Widened the verb alternation to `follow\s+(?:up\s+)?on` so both phrasings route through `#HandleCreateReminderFromTaskAboveAsync`, which correctly prepends the nearest preceding human thread message. Added 2 regression tests (`tests/reminders-app-mention-handler.test.js`, `TryHandleTaskAboveShorthandAsync` describe block) covering both phrasings; confirmed the new "follow on above" case fails against the pre-fix regex and passes after. Full suite green (1502/1502).

**Technical:** the startup message excerpt in `src/app.js` (`PostStartupNotificationAsync`) built its snippet with `TruncateToWords` on the *raw, whole* CHANGELOG.md file, so it could slice into the top-of-file authoring `<!-- HOW TO WRITE AN ENTRY -->` note instead of the newest `## ` block — unlike the `changelog` Slack command and the GH-408 tone validator, which both already skipped everything before the first `## ` heading. Extracted the shared block-parsing logic (previously only in `src/chat-commands/changelog-command.js`) into a new `src/changelog-parser.js` (`ExtractRecentVersionBlocks`) and added `BuildStartupChangelogExcerpt` to `src/startup-message.js` — the pure/testable layer — so all three consumers now go through one extraction implementation. Added `tests/startup-message.test.js` coverage: a fixture reproducing the exact leaked-note shape, a word-budget-truncation case, and an integration test that runs the excerpt builder against the live CHANGELOG.md and fails if `<!--`/`-->`/the authoring-note text ever appears in the output.

Had Sleuth create its own List instead — same `slackLists.create` + self-grant (`slackLists.access.set`) mechanism `lists-module.js` already uses for per-user reminder lists — which sidesteps that wall entirely and returns the real `column_id`s in the creation response instead of requiring a second lookup. Created list `F000EXAMPLE1` in the `neochrome` workspace (Name/Aliases/ChannelPatterns/RepoPatterns text columns), granted the requesting admin write access, and added the resulting `ClientsList` block (`ListId` + `Columns`) to [data/static/client-channel-mapping.json](data/static/client-channel-mapping.json) — the config `LoadClientsListConfigSync` already reads. `refresh clients` in `neochrome` now finds a real list instead of posting the "isn't wired up" setup help.

Added `client-f-canvas-wp-plugin` as a repo pattern on the existing Client F client, plus 5 new high-confidence entries (Client E, WP DB Toolkit, WP Code Check, Rebalance OS, AI-DDTK) to both the base config and the live Slack List, so the list isn't starting empty. Deliberately left out ~16 older, dormant (last active Feb-May 2026) side-project repos from the same log where the right client/project name or grouping wasn't obvious from commit messages alone (e.g. whether several repos are one product family, or how a few WP-plugin repos should be branded) — flagging those for a human pass rather than guessing at entries that would silently mistag future reminders. `tests/client-mapping.test.js` + `tests/refresh-clients-command.test.js` green (52). [package.json](package.json).

**Docs:** added a fenced `MANUAL SECTION: operator-clients-list` to [ARCHITECTURE.md](ARCHITECTURE.md) (under Lists Sync Pipeline) documenting the permission model in detail — the bot's `lists:write` scope is not the blocker a `list_not_found` error looks like; Slack Lists have a per-resource ACL independent of OAuth scope, so the bot needs either a human share (which can silently fail on a Slack-side "messages turned off" restriction, not a Sleuth bug) or to create the list itself and self-grant access, as done here for `F000EXAMPLE1`. Written to correct a prior session's mistaken "no write permission" conclusion.

## 1.4.236 - 2026-07-17
I can now actually hold a conversation in a DM! Message me directly and I'll reply — no need to @-mention me or start a thread first, and I'll still schedule reminders from a DM the same way I already do when you mention me. Follow-up replies in that DM (even ones that mention someone else) now get a reply too, instead of silently going quiet after the first one.

**Technical:** GH-412 (DM support gates). Root cause was three-fold: (1) `MessageEventInfo` (`src/slack-app.js`) silently stripped Slack's own `channel_type` field before it reached any handler, so nothing downstream could tell a DM apart from a channel message; (2) `reminders-module.js#OnMessageAsync`'s `AreRemindersEnabledForChannel` gate defaulted to an empty enabled-channels Set, so a DM channel was never in it; (3) `chat-module.js#ShouldRespondToMessageAsync` unconditionally required `thread_ts`, so a DM's first (always top-level) message was ignored. Fixed by threading `channel_type` through `MessageEventInfo` and bypassing both gates for `channel_type === 'im'` only (group DMs and channels untouched). **`/consult`-QA'd (agy + Codex)** before shipping: both independently caught two real bugs in the first pass — the chat bypass only covered a DM's first top-level message (threaded follow-ups fell back to the old hands-free logic and went silent), and the pre-existing `<@` mention check ran before the new DM branch (a DM that happened to mention another user was dropped). Both fixed by reordering the `channel_type === 'im'` check to run first, unconditionally, in `#ShouldRespondToMessageAsync`. 8 new regression tests across [tests/reminders-integration.test.js](tests/reminders-integration.test.js) and [tests/chat-module.integration.test.js](tests/chat-module.integration.test.js); `tests/mocks/mock-slack-app.js`'s `SimulateMessageAsync` updated to forward `channel_type`. Full suite green (1495/1495). Filed **GH-413** (admin-facing DM-reminders kill switch) as a deliberately deferred follow-up — adjudicated during the consult pass to ship without one now, not a blocker. [package.json](package.json).

## 1.4.230 - 2026-07-16
- **New (GH-397): `ROUTER_SHADOW_DEFAULT_MODE` env makes the router mode sticky across restarts/redeploys.** The GH-397 router mode (`off`/`shadow`/`active`) is per-workspace and in-memory, so every restart/redeploy previously reset it to the built-in `off` — meaning a production **observe/shadow** mode would silently fall back to `off` on the next deploy until an admin re-issued `@Sleuth AI router-mode shadow`. [src/router-shadow-module.js](src/router-shadow-module.js) now seeds `#Mode` at construction (startup) from `ROUTER_SHADOW_DEFAULT_MODE` via `#ResolveStartupMode()`: unset → built-in `off`; a valid `off|shadow|active` (case/space-insensitive) becomes the startup mode; an **invalid** value is warned and falls back to `off` (fail-safe — never arm on a typo), and a non-`off` startup is logged at info so the boot logs show the sticky default took effect. The runtime admin `router-mode` command still overrides the env at runtime; on the next restart the env default is re-applied (env is only the startup seed, not a runtime lock). Deliberately a single process-wide env applied at startup — a per-workspace persisted toggle stays out of scope. Docblock on [src/chat-commands/router-mode-command.js](src/chat-commands/router-mode-command.js) updated. New tests (unset→off, valid seeds mode, invalid→warned+off, runtime `SetMode` still overrides) in [tests/router-shadow.test.js](tests/router-shadow.test.js) — 22 green. GH-397 plan doc updated (persistence moved from non-goal to delivered). [package.json](package.json).

## 1.4.229 - 2026-07-16
- **New (GH-395 + GH-396): infer & inject the client name in front of synthesized tasks, fed by an operator-managed clients overlay.** Two related tickets on one change — #395 is the consumer, #396 is the data supply.
  - **GH-395 — client-name prefix.** Reminders in a client channel now read `Client E - Work on CR CC Tenant to Landlord workflow` instead of the bare task. This **reuses** the existing deterministic channel/repo resolver (`DoesReminderMatchClient` in [src/client-mapping.js](src/client-mapping.js)) rather than adding new inference — two small helpers were added: `ResolveClientNameForReminder` (returns the matched client's display name, or `null` on no confident match) and `ApplyClientPrefix` (prepends `"<Client> - "`, **idempotently** — never `Client A - Client A …` — and **fail-open**: no match → text unchanged, never a guessed client). Wired into the two chokepoints so every surface benefits: the render-time compact line (`BuildCompactTextForReminder` in [src/reminders-display-utils.js](src/reminders-display-utils.js), which flows through the GH-391 per-reminder poster → `ask-reminders`/buckets/digests) and the creation-time scheduling confirmation (`#ComposeFeedbackMessageText` in [src/reminders-module.js](src/reminders-module.js), the exact BEFORE/AFTER surface in the ticket). Display-only, non-destructive — stored reminder text is untouched, and the GH-391 render guard is not tripped.
  - **GH-396 — operator-managed clients list (Slack List → overlay).** So a non-engineer can add clients/aliases without a deploy. Extends the existing static/runtime split instead of inventing a store: [data/static/client-channel-mapping.json](data/static/client-channel-mapping.json) stays the engineer-owned **base**, and a new per-workspace **runtime overlay** (`data/runtime/client-mapping-overlay/<workspace>.json`) is merged on top by `ClientID`. `LoadClientMappingsSync(workspace)` now folds in that workspace's overlay (**extend-only** — the overlay may add net-new clients and extend alias/pattern arrays, but can never remove or override a curated base client, so a Slack typo can't un-wire one); `ReloadClientMappings(workspace)` busts the per-workspace cache after a sync. The cache is a `Map` keyed by workspace (never a single global) to honor the AGENTS.md §0.1 multi-tenant isolation contract. New `refresh clients` chat command ([src/chat-commands/refresh-clients-command.js](src/chat-commands/refresh-clients-command.js), registered in [src/chat-module.js](src/chat-module.js) + [data/static/ai/command-catalog.json](data/static/ai/command-catalog.json)) reads a **Slack List** (decision locked on #396; columns `Name | Aliases | ChannelPatterns | RepoPatterns`) via the Bolt Web client, normalizes rows to the mapping shape (`NormalizeClientRowsToOverlay`), writes the overlay (`WriteClientOverlaySync`), reloads the cache, and confirms `N clients loaded (M aliases)`. Since Slack Lists have no fetch-by-name, the list id + column→`column_id` map come from an optional `ClientsList` config block (`ListId`/`Columns`, or `SLEUTH_CLIENTS_LIST_ID` env), and the command posts actionable setup help when unconfigured. Fail-open throughout (missing/malformed overlay → base only; malformed rows skipped).
  - Tests: `ResolveClientNameForReminder`/`ApplyClientPrefix` (match, no-match, idempotency, regex-escape), base⊕overlay merge + reload + fail-open, `NormalizeClientRowsToOverlay`, `WriteClientOverlaySync`, `LoadClientsListConfigSync`, and the `refresh clients` command (field scalarize, paginated read, write+confirm, unconfigured, empty, error paths) in [tests/client-mapping.test.js](tests/client-mapping.test.js) + [tests/refresh-clients-command.test.js](tests/refresh-clients-command.test.js). Full suite green (85 suites / 1408 jest + 30 node); new/changed files `tsc`-clean; `validate:reminder-render`, `validate:workspace-isolation`, `validate:commands` (new route catalogued) all clean. [package.json](package.json).

## 1.4.228 - 2026-07-16
- **Fix (follow-on to GH-367 / GH-383): `ask-reminders` made a client's private-channel tasks visible to channel members via membership-aware need-to-know scoping.** Symptom: `@Sleuth what's open for Client D?` returned **"No matching tasks found"** despite open reminders and a correct client mapping. **Not a client-resolution bug** — `data/static/client-channel-mapping.json` already maps Client D, its `client-d` channel pattern matches, and the live reminders are stamped `clientId: "client-d"`. The tasks live in a **private/Slack-Connect channel** (`clients-1-client-d-external`), and the GH-367 channel-privacy scope (`IsVisibleUnderScope`, [src/reminder-query-engine.js](src/reminder-query-engine.js)) excludes private-channel candidates unless the channel is in `allowedChannelIds` — which previously held **only the channel the command ran in**. So a client's private-channel tasks were dropped before the client filter, when asked from anywhere else. The privacy MUST was correct for multi-tenant isolation but too coarse: it also hid the asking user's **own** private channels. **Fix:** a private candidate channel is now added to `allowedChannelIds` only when the **asking user is a confirmed member** of it (need-to-know) — non-members still never see a private channel's tasks, and the command channel stays always-allowed. New `SlackApp.IsUserChannelMemberAsync(channel, user)` (paginated `conversations.members`, `null`/fail-closed on error/inconclusive/page-cap) in [src/slack-app.js](src/slack-app.js); new per-`(channel,user)` `ChannelMembershipCache` (10-min TTL, mirrors `ChannelPrivacyCache`, only definitive booleans cached) in [src/channel-membership-cache.js](src/channel-membership-cache.js); `BuildMemberPrivateChannelSetAsync` expands the allowed set in [src/chat-commands/ask-reminders-command.js](src/chat-commands/ask-reminders-command.js), fail-closed when there's no asking user. The GH-367 query engine is unchanged (it still just honors `allowedChannelIds`); multi-tenant workspace isolation is untouched. New regression tests — member-sees-private, non-member-excluded, unresolved-fails-closed — in [tests/ask-reminders-command.test.js](tests/ask-reminders-command.test.js), plus a `ChannelMembershipCache` unit suite ([tests/channel-membership-cache.test.js](tests/channel-membership-cache.test.js)). ask-reminders + query-engine + candidates + privacy + membership suites green (54 tests); full suite 1383/1384 (the 1 failure is a pre-existing `development` thread-memory-embeddings test); `tsc` zero net-new errors; `validate:fsm`/`validate:ai` OK. Independent QA via a `/relay-xyz` round with **agy → PASS/Approved** (no blocking findings; transcript under `.xyz/relay-system/2026-07-16/`). [package.json](package.json).

## 1.4.227 - 2026-07-16
- **Fix (GH-391, cross-model consult review): cap the `ask-reminders` open render + harden the id-stripper and CI guard.** A Codex + agy `/consult` on the GH-391 diff surfaced a real regression a green suite missed. **(1) Blocker — channel flooding:** Phase 1 posts each open match as its own Slack message, but the open path had no cap (unlike `show-me`'s `SHOW_ME_TOP_N`), so a broad query (e.g. all of a busy client's tasks) would post dozens of messages and trip Slack rate limits. [ask-reminders-command.js](src/chat-commands/ask-reminders-command.js) now caps at `MAX_OPEN_REMINDERS_RENDERED = 15`, keeping the **soonest-due** when truncated and saying so (`Showing the 15 soonest-due of N open tasks — narrow it …`). **(2) `StripInternalReminderIds` hardening:** the regex missed a space after the colon (`id: abc`), left empty brackets (`[id:x]` → `[]`), and didn't `trimEnd`; now `/[[(]?\bid:\s*[A-Za-z0-9_-]+[\])]?[ \t]*/gi` handles all three (verified against the reviewers' own executed samples). **(3) Guard breadth ([validate-reminder-render.js](scripts/validate-reminder-render.js)):** added the string-concatenation vector (`'id:' + x`) to Shape 1, and fixed a Shape 2 **false positive** the review caught — the guard was flagging a *negated* instruction (`Do not cite reminder ids`), which is the correct guidance, so a negation exclusion was added. New regression tests for the cap, the stripper cases, and both guard changes in [tests/ask-reminders-command.test.js](tests/ask-reminders-command.test.js) + [tests/validate-reminder-render.test.js](tests/validate-reminder-render.test.js). (Consult transcripts under `relay-system/2026-07-16/gh391-qa-075133/`.) A separate pre-existing NaN time-window bypass the review flagged is tracked as its own follow-up issue, out of GH-391 scope. [package.json](package.json).

## 1.4.226 - 2026-07-16
- **New (GH-391 Phase 2): `validate:reminder-render` CI guard makes the reminder-render convention structural.** Docs are advisory — the same reason #384 shipped — so the reminder-render primitive from Phase 1 (1.4.225) is now enforced. [scripts/validate-reminder-render.js](scripts/validate-reminder-render.js) mirrors [scripts/validate-workspace-isolation.js](scripts/validate-workspace-isolation.js): scans `src/**/*.js`, reports `file:line`, exits non-zero, and honors an inline `// RENDER-OK: <reason>` pragma. It flags two shapes — **(1)** a standalone `id:${…}` construction (a raw reminder-id built into a string, the reported `id:fc3d9b07` blob shape), and **(2)** a prompt instructing the model to cite reminder ids (the exact GH-391 bad instruction), so neither can be reintroduced. Model-input renders (`RenderCitedCandidates` in [ask-reminders-command.js](src/chat-commands/ask-reminders-command.js), the extraction-prompt dedup summary in [reminders-ai-pipeline.js](src/reminders-ai-pipeline.js)) carry the reviewed pragma. Enforced under `npm test` via [tests/validate-reminder-render.test.js](tests/validate-reminder-render.test.js) (unit shapes + a "current src tree is clean" integration assertion). The guard surfaced and rejected a real false-positive during build — `code-task-relay-module.js`'s `<!-- task-id: ${…} -->` marker — so the Shape 1 pattern was tightened to a standalone `id:` token (negative lookbehind excludes `task-id:`/`msg-id:`). Documented in AGENTS.md §0.1 (the render rule + the guard family listed alongside the FSM and isolation guards). Added `npm run validate:reminder-render`. [package.json](package.json).

## 1.4.225 - 2026-07-16
- **Fix (GH-391 Phase 1): `ask-reminders` now renders open results as individual, reaction-actionable messages instead of a model-authored text blob with raw ids.** A session-built query path posted `what's open for Client A?` as one consolidated message with `id:fc3d9b07`-style debug prefixes (screenshot) — bypassing the canonical per-reminder renderer that `show-me`/the daily digest use, so tasks couldn't be ✅-completed or 🗑-deleted per item. Root cause: the handler fed the model an id-citing candidate render and *instructed it* to "Always cite reminder IDs" + "use bullet lists," then posted the model's single free-text answer. [src/chat-commands/ask-reminders-command.js](src/chat-commands/ask-reminders-command.js) now resolves matched **open** candidates back to their live `Reminder` objects and renders them through `PostBucketedReminderSectionsAsync` ([src/reminders-display-utils.js](src/reminders-display-utils.js)) — each its own message carrying the `sleuth-ai-reminder-ids` metadata the reaction-handler needs — with **no model call and no raw ids user-facing**. **Design note (Phase 0 finding):** the model had been doing open-vs-completed *intent resolution*, not just phrasing (the handler pulls all completed history into candidates too). So completed-history queries (no open matches) keep a concise model-phrased answer, but internal ids are stripped from the output (`StripInternalReminderIds` + a no-cite system prompt). Mixed results surface a "plus N already completed" note rather than silently dropping them. Tests in [tests/ask-reminders-command.test.js](tests/ask-reminders-command.test.js) now assert on the poster's per-reminder `ReminderID` metadata (not the model input), that no `id:` leaks to the channel, and cover the completed-history + id-strip paths. **Phase 2 (documented primitive + a `validate-reminder-render.js` CI guard mirroring the #387 isolation guard) is not in this change** — recommended to build via /relay. [package.json](package.json).

## 1.4.224 - 2026-07-16
- **Fix (GH-388): `ask-reminders` no longer fails silently when Slack reply posting itself breaks.** [src/chat-commands/ask-reminders-command.js](src/chat-commands/ask-reminders-command.js) now routes the empty-query and missing-reminders-module guard replies through the same protected path as the main handler, so `PostMessageTextAsync` failures there are caught too. The outer failure log now carries explicit `workspace=... channel=... command=ask-reminders` context, and the catch block's own failsafe reply is wrapped in its own `try/catch` so a second `channel_not_found`/`not_in_channel` failure cannot escape the handler. When that fails too, Sleuth emits an additional `error` log including the original error message and explicitly records that no DM/ephemeral fallback transport exists on the current `SlackApp` surface, rather than silently dying. Regression coverage in [tests/ask-reminders-command.test.js](tests/ask-reminders-command.test.js) forces `channel_not_found` on both the primary reply and the catch reply and asserts the handler resolves cleanly with the contextual logs present.
